- **Template bundles – set up together**: sets of templates that belong together (`examples/bundles/*.yaml`) – the processes that feed a dashboard, their recommended schedules and optional monitors – appear in the template gallery as _Set up together_. **Set up** asks for the platform to bind, creates the processes, the dashboard, the schedules and monitors **switched off**, starts one dry run per process and shows the result with links and a **Turn on** step. Re-running it keeps or updates (_Update to the template_) what exists; schedules and monitors that exist are kept. Audited as `template_bundle.setup` and `template_bundle.turned_on`. Bundles for Insights Hub access governance and tenant usage, Snowflake, Git repositories, AWS, Prometheus service levels, Kubernetes and Dependency-Track ([examples/README.md](examples/README.md#template-bundles)); `npm run validate` checks them. API: `GET /api/template-bundles`, `POST /api/template-bundles/:id/setup`, `POST /api/template-bundles/:id/turn-on`.
- **Platform setup wizard**: _Add platform_ leads through four steps – choose a connector (search, grouped by area), address and only the credentials it needs (the auth type follows the fields filled in), a **live connection test** with the diagnosis and the usual fixes inline (_Change the settings_, _Test again_), and **what's next**: matching bundles and templates, monitor check types and examples, access checks, the MCP gateway. _Skip the wizard_ keeps the quick form ([docs/PLATFORM-SIGN-IN.md](docs/PLATFORM-SIGN-IN.md#adding-a-platform)).
- **Platform health**: the platform page shows the last connection test – result, time, who ran it, message – prominently, and a **periodic connection test** (off by default, every 15 minutes to once a day) whose failures notify the platform's managers with the new event **Platform connection failing**, once per failing period and routed like other notifications. A failed test lists the usual fixes. The tab **Get started** shows what's next with the platform. API: `GET/PUT /api/platforms/:id/health`, `GET /api/platforms/:id/get-started`.
- **Kafka – Add topics by prefix**: on an application, a Kafka platform and a prefix (`smo-ie.dev.`) list the cluster's topics under it (read-only); the chosen ones become Kafka topic components with an environment. **Keep in sync** offers new topics under the prefix on the application page and in the daily summary (new section _New Kafka topics_) until they are added or dismissed ([docs/KAFKA.md](docs/KAFKA.md#add-topics-by-prefix)).
- **Confluent Cloud access reviews**: the platform preset _Confluent Cloud API_ (`https://api.confluent.cloud`, Cloud API key, basic auth) and the access check source **Confluent Cloud role bindings and ACLs** – the IAM role bindings under an organisation, environment or cluster CRN and, with the cluster's REST endpoint and API key, its Kafka ACLs; users are named by their e-mail address and service accounts by id, so person reviews include Confluent ([docs/CONFLUENT.md](docs/CONFLUENT.md)).
- **Duplicate** – the same action with the same rules for monitors, processes, dashboards, credentials register entries, applications, services, schedules, report schedules, maintenance windows, access checks and status pages (a button on detail pages, an entry in list rows). Secrets are never copied (heartbeat secrets, webhook trigger secrets, platform credentials, status page links, secret schedule inputs, MCP tokens): the copy gets new ones or none. A free name and key are suggested (`… (copy)`, `…-copy`, `…-copy-2`) and checked before saving. The copy belongs to the person who makes it and gets their default owning teams; the original's access list, owners and owning teams are copied only for who may manage the original (with a step-up), so duplicating never widens anyone's access. History, runs, results and the audit trail stay with the original; schedules, report schedules, maintenance windows and access checks start disabled. The copy shows _Copied from …_ and is audited as `<entity>.duplicated` with the original's id. Applications take their components, _runs on_ systems and documents along as chosen (never their platform mappings – a platform belongs to one application), services their applications and documents. API: `POST /api/<kind>/:id/duplicate`, `GET /api/duplicates/:kind/:id` (the dialog's suggestion) and `GET /api/duplicates/:kind/:id/source`. See [docs/TEAMS.md](docs/TEAMS.md#duplicating).
- **Monitor editor: duplicate several checks at once** – shift-click or shift-drag a box around cards, _Duplicate selection_ copies them with the links between them. **Save as template** turns a saved monitor into a monitor template; you choose which fields become parameters (host names, URLs, asset ids and keys preselected), their current values are the defaults ([docs/MONITORS.md](docs/MONITORS.md#templates-for-many-machines)).
- **Maintenance windows can be disabled** (_Enabled_ in the form): a disabled window neither applies nor announces anything until it is enabled; a copy starts so. The public API leaves disabled windows out, as cancelled ones. Migrations `duplicates-001-copied-from` and `duplicates-002-maintenance-enabled` (additive).
- **Person reviews** show the source's hint next to each account in the reviewer's language – the last login and _Password without MFA_ (Snowflake) – and the e-mail address the account was found by (`subjectActivityInfo`, `matchedAliases` on review items).
- **Architecture documentation:** [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) with C4 context and container diagrams (Mermaid) and a module map of `server/src`, and decision records in [docs/adr/](docs/adr/README.md) – the synchronous database layer with the PostgreSQL bridge, one active instance with a lease lock, the HMAC-chained audit log with signed checkpoints, the vault with a master passphrase, route access declared on routes, and owning teams in one policy module.
- **Audit log epochs, archives and retention.** Every signed checkpoint closes an epoch. Under _Audit log → Epochs, archives and retention_ (off by default) administrators turn on **archiving of closed epochs**: once a month of checkpoints is over, its entries go to `<data dir>/audit-archives/` as a signed, self-verifying ZIP (entries as JSON lines, the epoch checkpoints, a manifest with SHA-256 of every file signed with the installation's Ed25519 key), optionally also uploaded to the S3 target of the backups, encrypted with the backup key. With a **retention period** (off by default, at least 30 days) the daily cleanup deletes archived entries older than it from the database, in batches – only when the archive file is there unchanged (and uploaded) – after writing a deletion record to the chain; entries that records, access checks, reports and run protocols rely on stay. The chain still verifies from the signed checkpoint of the newest deleted archive, the JSON export carries that checkpoint (`startCheckpoint`; `audit:verify-export` continues from it), and evidence packs over a deleted period carry the period's archives and verify them offline. Every archive, upload, download and deletion is audited. Migration `audit-001-archives` (table `audit_archives`, index on `audit_log(action, seq)`).
- **`steward audit verify-archive <file> …`** (`npm run audit:verify-archive --`) checks audit archives offline – signature (`--public-key`, `--fingerprint` or `--trust-pack-key`), files, the chain and every checkpoint, and that several archives follow on from each other; exit status as for `evidence verify`.
- **Full verification of the audit chain in the background** with progress (_Audit log → Epochs, archives and retention → Full verification_, administrators; `POST /api/audit/verify/full`), and on its own every `SM_AUDIT_FULL_VERIFY_DAYS` (default 7). It also checks the archive files against their recorded SHA-256. The page shows the epochs, what is archived and deleted, and the last incremental and full verification (`GET /api/audit/archive`, auditors and administrators).
- **Background jobs on System status.** The server's periodic work – reminders, service duties, the scorecard, the integrations of applications (Kubernetes, repositories, Kafka, AWS, Snowflake, Dependency-Track), daily summaries, the clean-up, audit checkpoints and the Git sync – runs in one job runner: a job never runs twice at the same time (a start while it still runs is skipped and counted), each has a time limit, and its last start, end, duration, result and error are kept in the database across restarts. Administrators see a table with the next run and **Run now** (audited as `job.run_requested`); `/api/metrics` has `sm_job_last_success_timestamp_seconds`, `sm_job_duration_seconds` and `sm_job_failures_total` per job (`GET /api/system/jobs`, `POST /api/system/jobs/<name>/run`).
- **Missed schedule times are reported.** Times a schedule was due while Steward was not running (or its vault was locked) are found at the next start: one message _Missed while Steward was not running_ per schedule to its owner and the administrators, an audit entry `schedule.missed` and a badge on the schedule. The new schedule option **Catch up after a restart** (`catchUp: 'once'`, default `none`) starts one run right after the start (audited as `schedule.caught_up`). `/api/v1` schedules carry `catchUp` and `missed`.
- **A limit for runs at the same time**, `SM_MAX_CONCURRENT_RUNS` (default 20, `0`: none). Further runs wait in status _queued_ – _Waiting for a free slot_ on the run's page – and start first come, first served when a run ends or begins waiting for a decision; queued runs survive a restart and can be cancelled.
- **Event-loop lag** as a metric (`sm_event_loop_lag_seconds`, p99 over 10 s) and a warning in the log when a request had to wait longer than `SM_EVENT_LOOP_WARN_MS` (default 1000 ms).

### Changed

- **Sidebar**: _Service management_ is now _Catalogue_ and _Administration_ is _Admin_. Schedules, Triggers and Git sync left the sidebar for tabs above Processes (their addresses stay); System status has its own entry for administrators and auditors, so auditors no longer see _Settings_. Folded and opened areas are remembered per user in this browser (before: for everyone using the browser).
- **Export packages**: _Processes → Export all processes & dashboards_ and _Import package_ (formerly _bundle_, in German already _Paket_) so they are not mixed up with the new template bundles; the API (`/api/bundle/…`, format `steward-bundle`) stays.
- **Daily summary**: a section left out on purpose stays out when later releases add sections (settings saved since 0.4.0 remember the sections there were); settings saved before keep today's behaviour.
- **Connection tests** record their message in the audit entry (`platform.tested` → `details.message`), so the platform page shows it later.
- **The switch that allows daily summaries** (_Settings → Notifications_) is saved with the save bar like the other settings instead of at once.
- **Docs** say _documents_ and _document types_ where the web app does (formerly _records_); API routes and fields keep the name `records`.
  - the run page's main button is _Run report_ (was _Protocol_) and its _Log_ tab _Messages_; in German _Ausführungsbericht_ and _Meldungen_ (both were _Protokoll_);
  - _Records_ are called _Documents_ in English as in German (_Dokumente_), with _Document types_; _Tickets_ in the sidebar is _Ticket references_ as on its page;
  - the start page is _Home_ in English, so it no longer reads like _Dashboards_;
  - _Updates from Git_ is _Git sync_ in the sidebar and _Git sync updates_ on its page, one name with the sources in the settings (German _Git-Synchronisierung_ throughout);
  - the settings area _Maintenance_ (retention, cleanup, backups) is _Data & retention_ at `/settings/data`, so it no longer collides with the maintenance windows in the sidebar; `/settings/maintenance` leads there;
  - _Settings → General → Unattended operation_ names each server option in plain words, with its environment variable (`SM_*`) as the detail;
  - the description of person reviews says in plain words what happens (snapshot of the rights, a decision per right, approval by a second person, no changes afterwards).
- **Configuration is checked at start-up.** Every environment variable the server reads is declared once, with its type, limits and default, in `server/src/config-schema.ts` and validated when the server starts: an invalid value (a port that is not a number, an unknown `SM_API_DOCS` mode, a session length out of range) refuses to start with one line per bad variable instead of being ignored or replaced by the default somewhere deep in the code. Names and defaults are unchanged. The variable tables in the README and the new [docs/CONFIGURATION.md](docs/CONFIGURATION.md) are generated from the schema (`npm run config:docs`; CI checks them with `--check`). **Check before updating:** values that were silently ignored before now stop the start – for example `SM_PASSWORD_LOGIN`, `SM_API_DOCS` or `SM_OUTBOUND_POLICY` with an unknown value, `SM_API_RATE_LIMIT`/`SM_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MINUTE` that are not whole numbers, `SM_AUDIT_CHECKPOINT_MINUTES` below 5 or on/off variables with a value other than 1/0, true/false, on/off, yes/no. Start once with the new version in a shell to see the list.
- **The container image runs compiled JavaScript.** `npm run build` now also compiles the server with esbuild into `server/dist/` (`npm run build:server`); the image starts `node --enable-source-maps server/dist/index.js` and its health check `node server/dist/healthcheck.js`, without TypeScript sources and without the `tsx` loader, which is now a development dependency. The PDF worker and the command-line tools are compiled next to it (`node server/dist/admin.js`, `migrate.js`, `backup.js`, `db-copy.js`, …). `npm start` and `npm run dev` still run the sources; `npm run start:dist` runs the compiled server. CI starts the compiled server once per build (`scripts/smoke-server.mjs`). **Check before updating:** scripts that run tools in the container with `node --import tsx server/src/cli/<tool>.ts` use `node server/dist/<tool>.js` instead ([docs/INSTALL.md](docs/INSTALL.md#4-container)).
- **Errors of the internal API are problem details.** Every error answer under `/api` (the web UI's API) is now `application/problem+json` (RFC 9457) with `type` (`urn:steward:error:<code>` or `about:blank`), `title`, `status`, `detail` and `instance`, like `/api/v1` – from the route handlers, the error handler and the request guards (sign-in, host check, CSRF header, body limits) alike. `error`, `code`, `params` and the other members stay as extension members, and every problem names its `requestId`, so the web UI works unchanged. [docs/API.md](docs/API.md#the-internal-api) now says plainly that the internal API is private and may change in any release, and that `/api/v1` is the public contract.
- **Dropped errors say why.** An ESLint rule (`scripts/eslint/catch-reason.js`) requires a reason in every empty `catch` block and next to every `.catch(() => undefined)`; the 60 places without one got a short reason, and the ones that hid real failures now log them: a failing Git sync tick (`git sync tick failed`), a Git sync re-run after a webhook and a failure screenshot that could not be saved (warnings in the server log). Behaviour is otherwise unchanged.
- **The roadmap is current again** ([docs/ROADMAP.md](docs/ROADMAP.md)): what shipped up to 0.3 (status pages, incident reviews and maintenance windows were still listed as parked or out of scope), the themes of 0.4.0 and later, and explicit non-goals – no on-call or paging, no CMDB or discovery, no metrics stack, no secret storage or rotation for others, no full GRC suite, no AI changes without human approval.
- **Development: query keys from one place.** The web app takes the TanStack Query keys of runs, processes, platforms, monitors, dashboards, datasets and schedules from per-feature factories in `web/src/queryKeys.ts` instead of about 150 inline arrays, so a list and its invalidations cannot drift apart. The keys themselves are unchanged.
- **Verifying the audit chain is incremental**: _Verify chain_, `GET /api/audit/verify`, `GET /api/v1/audit/verify` and report verification check the entries after the newest signed checkpoint (each one is signed only after the entries up to it verified) instead of every entry from #1, so they no longer slow down as the log grows; evidence packs continue the digest from the newest checkpoint as well. `?full=true` (and a pasted checkpoint) checks every entry; after a failed full verification every verification is a full one until one passes again. The results carry `mode` (`incremental` or `full`), `fromSeq` and `deletedThroughSeq`. **Check before updating:** a script that relies on `/api/v1/audit/verify` checking the whole log should pass `full=true` (or rely on the scheduled full verification).
- **Datasets are stored once.** Publishing a dataset no longer reads and parses the rows of every partition again to write a combined copy into `datasets.rows`: the rows live in their partition only, and readers (dashboards, `/api/v1` datasets, MCP `read_dataset`, access reconciliation, Prometheus) put them together when they read, with recently parsed partitions kept in memory (`SM_DATASET_CACHE_ROWS`, default 100,000 rows) so paging through `/api/v1/datasets/{key}/rows` parses a partition once. Republishing a 20,000-row partition next to another one took about 4× less time in the test (median ≈ 38 ms instead of ≈ 170 ms) and no longer writes the 6.6 MB copy. Migration `ops-014-dataset-rows-in-parts` frees the old copies (the column stays, `'[]'`).
- **The retention cleanup works in small batches** – at most 500 rows per statement with a turn for the event loop in between, run by run – so deleting many old runs with large logs, read notifications or archived audit entries no longer blocks requests.
- **Clean stop on SIGTERM/SIGINT.** Steward no longer exits at once: it stops being ready, starts no new runs (`503 SHUTTING_DOWN`) and no scheduled fires, stops report schedules, access checks, backups, background jobs and monitors, gives running steps up to `SM_SHUTDOWN_GRACE_SECONDS` (default 25) to finish, marks what still runs as interrupted, then closes and flushes logs and error reports. Runs waiting for a person are not waited for; they continue after the restart. The compose files set `stop_grace_period: 30s` (Docker's default is 10 s); see _Stopping and restarting_ in docs/INSTALL.md.
- **PostgreSQL: a second instance waits instead of exiting**, and a crashed one no longer blocks the start. The single-instance check is a lease row (`sm_instance_lease`, renewed every 10 s, valid for 45 s) instead of only the advisory lock: a stopped instance hands over at once, a crashed one's lease expires on its own, and the waiting instance answers `/api/health` with 200 and `/api/ready` with 503 until it takes over – no crash loop in Kubernetes and no `pg_terminate_backend` by hand. The advisory lock is still held, so 0.3 and 0.4 never run side by side.
- **Start-up and shutdown order in one place** (`server/src/bootstrap.ts`): recovery of runs, scheduler, monitors and the other services that need the vault start on unlock in a fixed order and stop in reverse; the former start-up file with the warning filter is now `server/src/warnings.ts`.

### Fixed

- **Demo setup and _Use template_ are fast again.** Since 0.3.1 every process created from a template read all process and dashboard templates again (for the gallery's metadata), so loading the demo data took about twice as long and the CI test runs hit their time limit. A template is now read from its own file only.
- **Snowflake `390144 JWT token is invalid`** now names the usual causes (the account identifier the JWT needs, the user, the public key) in a database component's readout and in the Snowflake monitor check too, not only in the connection test.

## [0.3.1] - 2026-11-03

Integrations now say what went wrong: a connection test no longer calls a sign-in page or redirect a working connection, credentials entered but never sent are pointed out, and GitLab, Prometheus, AWS and Snowflake errors name the likely cause. GitLab is called with `PRIVATE-TOKEN`, Prometheus with POST queries, the AWS test checks every permission Steward needs, and process `http` steps can call AWS services and have an answer size limit. Dataset freshness of scheduled runs follows the schedule as it is now, per source, in words. A run whose checks failed shows **Completed with findings** instead of a green _Succeeded_. Templates arrive for Snowflake, Git hosts, AWS and Prometheus, with a searchable template gallery. A round of accessibility, wording and mobile fixes rounds it off. One database migration, additive: `ops-013-dataset-stale-fixed`. **Check before updating:** GitLab platforms that use OAuth tokens need the bearer header name `Authorization`; publish steps whose `staleAfterMinutes` should stay a fixed limit on scheduled processes need `staleAfterFixed: true`; some labels changed (_Records_ → _Documents_, _Protocol_ → _Run report_, _Updates from Git_ → _Git sync_), the addresses stay.

### Added

- **Prometheus platforms bring an API description** of the read endpoints (`query`, `query_range`, `series`, labels, metadata, targets, alerts, rules), so assistants run PromQL through the MCP gateway and process steps find the operations. Platforms made before add it under _API specs → Add the built-in specification_ (`POST /api/platforms/{id}/specs/built-in`, for every preset that brings one).
- **Docs:** how to describe an AWS account shared by several applications and a Confluent environment (`docs/APPLICATIONS.md`).
- **Snowflake – Project access review** (example 61): the way a project's access is reviewed by hand – `SHOW GRANTS ON DATABASE` for each database, `SHOW GRANTS OF ROLE` for each role with a privilege, nested roles followed up to four levels to the users behind them, with the path; users from `ACCOUNT_USAGE.USERS` (type, disabled, last login, e-mail, password and MFA), technical users marked by type or a name pattern; recently active people and technical users preselected; four-eyes before the revocation, a manual step with the `REVOKE ROLE … FROM USER …;` statements (the connection is read-only) verified by a second person, access changes documented; dataset `snowflake.project-access`. Database and role names are checked and always sent as quoted identifiers ([docs/SNOWFLAKE.md](docs/SNOWFLAKE.md#templates)).
- **Snowflake – Account hygiene and usage** (example 62, daily): people inactive for 90 days, password sign-ins without MFA, technical users with a password, ACCOUNTADMIN and SECURITYADMIN holders, disabled users that still hold roles, warehouse credits over 7 and 30 days, failed tasks – datasets `snowflake.users`, `snowflake.credits`, `snowflake.tasks`, the dashboard _Snowflake account_ and the monitor example _Snowflake account_ (freshness, failing tasks, credits).
- **Git – Member and outside-collaborator review** (example 92, quarterly) for GitHub, GitLab and Forgejo / Gitea in one process: owners, members, outside collaborators, two-factor authentication (GitHub `filter=2fa_disabled`, GitLab for administrators) and the last activity where the host offers it (GitLab billable members, Forgejo last login); attest, four-eyes, manual removal with maker–checker, access changes in the rights of the source _Git_; dataset `git.members` ([docs/GIT-PLATFORMS.md](docs/GIT-PLATFORMS.md#templates)).
- **Git – Repository health for GitLab and Forgejo** (example 82, formerly _GitHub – repository health_): the same process now reads GitHub, GitLab or Forgejo – whichever platform is bound – and adds whether the default branch is protected and the GitLab deploy and access tokens that expire soon; one schedule per host, one part per host in `git.repositories`, so the _Repositories_ dashboard shows all three. The connection slot keeps its name `github`; GitHub rows keep their keys.
- **AWS – IAM access key age and unused credentials** (example 63, daily) and **AWS – Costs per application** (example 64, monthly), from the cloud accounts of the applications – datasets `aws.iam-keys`, `aws.costs` and the dashboard _Cloud costs and keys_ ([docs/AWS.md](docs/AWS.md#templates)).
- **Prometheus – SLO and error-budget report** (example 65, weekly): availability per service over 28 days from your PromQL (`$window`), error budget left, burn rate over 1 h and 6 h, fast burn – dataset `prom.slo`, the dashboard _Service levels_ and the monitor example _Error budget burn rate_.
- **Monitor examples** for the check types that had none: Snowflake, Prometheus, Kubernetes, Git, Kafka, MQTT, CheckMk and a TCP service template (`examples/monitors/50`–`57`), with generic platform names that find the platform of the kind on import.
- **Template metadata**: `GET /api/templates` returns per template the platform kinds it needs (`connections`, `connectionKinds`), the recommended schedule (`recommendedSchedule: { cron, text }`, from an optional `recommendedSchedule` field or the description), the datasets it publishes, the dashboards it feeds and its `minVersion` – for searching and filtering the template gallery. A connection slot may name several fitting kinds with the optional hint `kinds` ([examples/README.md](examples/README.md#template-metadata)).
- **Snowflake access source**: reads `EMAIL`, `LAST_SUCCESS_LOGIN`, `HAS_PASSWORD` and `HAS_MFA` where the account's USERS view has them (the old columns otherwise). Person reviews find Snowflake users by their e-mail address and show the last login as a hint next to each right; the hint is not part of the snapshots. Existing Snowflake checks take one snapshot with the e-mail addresses on their next run, without findings.
- **Demo**: the sample applications _Webshop (demo)_ and _CRM (demo)_ with a repository, a Kubernetes namespace, Kafka topics, Snowflake databases and AWS cloud accounts (read once at setup, with end-of-life data); feeders for CheckMk, Kubernetes, the three Git hosts, Dependency-Track, Snowflake, Prometheus, end of life, Kafka and AWS, so every example dashboard has data; access checks of Snowflake, AWS and GitHub, so person reviews span several platforms. The Snowflake, Git and Prometheus mocks answer what the new templates read. `POST /api/demo/setup` with `{ "applications": false }` leaves out the sample applications.

### Changed

- **Heartbeat checks show the last report received in the editor**, right above the condition and the status expression, so they can be written against the fields that actually arrive.
- **Run status and findings are kept apart.** A run that executed every step but whose checks failed no longer shows a green _Succeeded_ next to "3 failed steps": it shows **Completed with findings** (amber) in the run list, the run page, the start page, process cards, the HTML protocol and the run report PDF, and its failed checks are called **findings** with their severity ("2 findings (1 error, 1 warning)"); _failed steps_ now count only steps that failed to execute. The run page lists the findings at the top, each with what was found first and the expectation (the check's name) after it. The stored status keeps its meaning (`succeeded`): the outcome is derived. API: runs in `/api` and `/api/v1` gain `outcome` (`findings` or the status) and `findings` (`total`, `error`, `warning`, `info`), `lastRun.outcome` on processes, MCP runs gain `outcome` and `findings`; the Runs page's status filter offers _Completed with findings_ (`GET /api/runs?outcome=findings|succeeded`).
- **Start page.** A tile **Runs with findings (24 h)** next to _Failed runs (24 h)_. Tiles of features nobody set up yet say **Not set up yet** (neutral, after the others; on phones folded into one line) instead of a reassuring "0 · All quiet"; the attention counts gain `total` (`checks` for access checks) for that. The failed-checks panel is now **Check findings**: each entry leads with the result ("1 of 7 endpoint(s) down: plant/Historian API") and shows the expectation under it. Auditors get a **compliance start page** (access changes, reviews, runs with findings, duties, credentials, scorecard, person reviews, evidence packs, controls, audit log) and viewers one with their **dashboards**; _Show the full operations start page_ switches (remembered per user).
- **Process templates**: the gallery has a search, a filter by platform kind, _Matches your platforms_, groups by the platform a template needs, a **needs: <kind> platform** badge per required connection (and the recommended schedule when the server names one), and after _Use_ offers the next steps – a dry run, a schedule (preselected) or the editor. The Processes header keeps _From template_ and _New process_; a manual procedure, YAML import and bundles moved into **More**, with what a bundle is.
- **New dashboard** opens a dialog – name, owning teams (administrators; others see the teams their new resources get), blank or from a template – and lands in edit mode instead of saving a dashboard called "New dashboard".
- **Empty dashboard widgets** say in plain words, by role, what fills them: viewers read "No data yet – ask a designer to schedule _Privileged access review_", people who may run or edit the publishing process get links to it and to a new schedule, with the dataset key as a detail. The publishing process comes from the process definitions (`GET /api/datasets/{key}/publishers`), so it is known before the first run.
- **The getting-started checklist folds** to its progress and the next step – folded by default on phones, remembered per user – and opens again from the user menu.
- The daily summary counts runs that completed with findings ("12 runs, 0 failed, 9 completed with findings").
- **The sidebar leaves out pages a role cannot use**: viewers and approvers no longer see _Schedules_ and _Triggers_, people without a role that reads the credentials register (and without an entry of their own) no longer see _Credentials_, and _Settings_ shows for administrators and auditors only (everybody else has their profile in the user menu). Roles from a team count. The addresses keep working. `GET /api/auth/status` carries two additional capabilities for it, `viewAutomation` and `viewCredentials`.
- **Clearer names** (labels only, every address stays):
  - the run page's main button is _Run report_ (was _Protocol_) and its _Log_ tab _Messages_; in German _Ausführungsbericht_ and _Meldungen_ (both were _Protokoll_);
  - _Records_ are called _Documents_ in English as in German (_Dokumente_), with _Document types_; _Tickets_ in the sidebar is _Ticket references_ as on its page;
  - the start page is _Home_ in English, so it no longer reads like _Dashboards_;
  - _Updates from Git_ is _Git sync_ in the sidebar and _Git sync updates_ on its page, one name with the sources in the settings (German _Git-Synchronisierung_ throughout);
  - the settings area _Maintenance_ (retention, cleanup, backups) is _Data & retention_ at `/settings/data`, so it no longer collides with the maintenance windows in the sidebar; `/settings/maintenance` leads there;
  - _Settings → General → Unattended operation_ names each server option in plain words, with its environment variable (`SM_*`) as the detail;
  - the description of person reviews says in plain words what happens (snapshot of the rights, a decision per right, approval by a second person, no changes afterwards).
- **One way to save settings**: every settings area ends with one save bar – _Unsaved changes_, _Reset_ and _Save_ – that stays at the bottom of the window while something is unsaved and saves every changed card of the area (instance settings, ticket sources, notification channels and their look, credential reminders, appearance, report branding). The cards' own save buttons in between (a grey _Save_ between the cards on _General_, _Save ticket settings_ inside a card) are gone. The buttons that open a page of their own (_Manage Git sync_, _Open migration_, backups) look alike.
- **Controls are grouped by the catalogue's sections** (ISO/IEC 27001 5–8, IEC 62443-2-1 4.2–4.4), each with its counts and a coverage bar, folded while nothing in it is mapped. The summary says how many controls have evidence and counts controls without mapped items as _not mapped – not assessed yet_, with a sentence on what that means, instead of a grey chip next to _0 gaps_. The period shows its dates in the app's format (_5 Aug 2026 – 3 Nov 2026_); the browser's date fields appear for a custom period only.
- **Phones: users and dashboard tables as cards**: _Users & teams_ shows one card per person (name, user name and e-mail, the roles in full, teams, last sign-in, active switch) instead of a table that cut the roles to "AP…" and clipped _Last sign-in_; the desktop table can be sorted by name, teams and last sign-in. A dashboard's table widget shows one card per row on phones, with the identifying column as its title, the status beside it and the source last, instead of putting _Source_ first and cutting off the e-mail.
- **Process editor**: _Full screen_ folds the app sidebar away for more room (back to normal when the editor closes); the flow opens at a readable zoom (step names at least 11 px – it used to shrink a large flow to about 5 px) with the Start node in view, and the minimap gives the overview; the palette is wider and shows every step's full name (_User task / approval_, _Publish to dashboard_); a step's properties show the basic settings first and fold the node id, unset options, outputs, conditions and retries under _Advanced_. On a phone the flow is read-only, with the note _Open on a larger screen to edit_.
- **Platforms list**: a search and filter row (name, URL or description; kind; environment), the latest connection test on every card as a status dot – _Last test OK 5 minutes ago_, _Last test failed …_ or _Connection not tested yet_ – and the sign-in kind in normal case (_OAuth2 client credentials_, not all caps). `GET /api/platforms` carries `lastTest` (`{ at, ok }` or `null`), taken from the audit log's `platform.tested` entries, which now record the result in their details; nothing is added to the database.
- **Dataset freshness follows the schedule**: a partition written by a run that a schedule started (or a sub-process of such a run) is stale after 2.5 × the schedule's interval (at least 15 minutes); the publish step's `staleAfterMinutes` applies to runs without a schedule. Before, `staleAfterMinutes` won for every run, so a weekly dataset with the examples' 180 minutes showed as stale – and sent stale messages – three hours after each run. To keep the step's limit for scheduled runs too, set the new optional `staleAfterFixed: true` on the publish step (_Use “Stale after” for scheduled runs too_). Check processes whose limit was meant to be stricter or more tolerant than 2.5 × their schedule. Migration `ops-013-dataset-stale-fixed` (one nullable column); existing partitions follow their schedule right away. The `sm_dataset_stale` Prometheus gauge uses the same rule.
- **Process `http` steps can call AWS APIs**: on an AWS account platform, `aws: { service, region }` on the step sends the request to that service's endpoint (`https://<service>.<region>.amazonaws.com`, the path relative to it) signed with the platform's SigV4 keys or role – for AWS APIs Steward has no connector for. The outbound policy counts that endpoint as the platform's own origin only for a step that names the service. See [AWS](docs/AWS.md#aws-calls-in-processes).

### Fixed

- **Monitor editor: a check id taken by accident could not be corrected.** A new card that got the id of an already saved check (typed, or after duplicating) showed _duplicate check id_ but its id field was locked as _Fixed once saved_. Only the saved check keeps its id fixed now; the new card's id stays editable.
- **Monitor editor: _Duplicate_ could place the copy on top of another card**, hiding it; the copy now goes to the first free place next to or below the original.
- **MCP gateway tools were missing until the client reconnected**: MCP clients keep the tool list of their first connection, and the gateway tools (`platform_find_operations`, `platform_describe_operation`, `platform_call_operation`) only appeared once a platform had opened its gateway – a client connected before that never saw them. They are now always listed; a call without a platform the token owner may use through the gateway says how to get one (turn it on under _Platform → MCP_, the `use` right).
- A new service without applications and documents was flagged _Attention needed_ at once: its protection need and documentation are now _Open_ until there is something to judge.
- Process and template descriptions showed raw Markdown (`**webhook trigger**`, backticks) and broke lines mid-sentence: they are running text with bold, italics and code.
- In a dashboard's edit mode the empty state said "Click Edit and add widgets"; it now points at _Add widget_ above.
- **Accessibility (WCAG 2.2 AA)**: every page title is now the page's level-1 heading (it was a level-2 heading, so pages had no `h1`; the size is unchanged), a _Skip to content_ link is the first element reached with the Tab key (visible while focused) and moves the focus to the page content, icon-only buttons are at least 24 × 24 px (small ones 28 px; _Download CSV_, _Undo_/_Redo_ were 22 px) and the sidebar's section headings 28 px high, and a run's result in run lists shows a warning icon next to the red text of failed checks and steps. The axe tests also check for one `h1` per page and the target size of buttons.
- **First-run setup**: the length rule of the master passphrase (at least 12 characters) is shown under the field and counts down while you type, with an error once you leave the field too short and when the repeated passphrase differs – it used to be a note under the disabled _Set up_ button.
- **Dataset freshness reads the schedule as it is now**: the interval and the stale limit of a partition come from its schedule's current cron when the dataset is read, so changing a schedule shows at once instead of after the next run (the interval stored at the last run is kept for a deleted schedule). Every source now carries its own `nextRunAt` (the next run of the schedule that feeds it, or none), `staleReason` (`schedule`, `fixed` or `default`), the schedule's name and – for a sub-process – the parent process whose schedule started it; `GET /api/v1/datasets…` adds `staleReason` and `nextRunAt` per source.
- **Freshness widget**: shows each source on its own lines with a status chip (_Fresh_, _Due_ when a scheduled run is overdue, _Stale since …_), the interval in words (_hourly_, _every 15 minutes_, _daily_, _weekly_, _monthly_, _quarterly_, else e.g. _every 3 days 4 h_ instead of _1440 min_), when it counts as stale and why (_stale after 2 h 30 min (from the schedule “Inventory EU”)_, _via the “Nightly checks” schedule_ for a sub-process, _fixed, run without a schedule_), and the next refresh of **that source's** schedule – before, the earliest schedule of the process was shown for every source, so with one schedule per tenant none matched. The lines wrap on a phone instead of being cut off in a sideways-scrolling table, and the dashboard's source filter applies.
- **Cron preview warns about the day-of-month/day-of-week trap**: like vixie cron, croner runs on a day when **either** field matches once both are restricted, so `0 6 1-7 * 1` (“the first Monday”) ran on every day 1–7 and on every Monday. `POST /api/cron/preview` now returns `warnings`, and the editors of schedules, report schedules, backups (and their restore test) and access checks show them under the cron field with the nth-weekday form (`0 6 * * 1#1`) to use instead. Report schedules, backups and access checks now show the next runs there too.
- **Unexpected errors no longer show their message** (OWASP ASVS V7.4.1): a 500 that is not a deliberate error answered with the error's text, so an internal `TypeError` or an upstream address could reach the browser. It now answers `{"error": "Internal error (request id …)", "code": "INTERNAL_ERROR", "params": {"requestId": …}}` and the web app shows _Something went wrong (reference …)_; the full error goes to the request's log line. Permission, validation and platform errors keep their message. 5xx answers of `/api/v1` are reported to error reporting too.
- **Request ids**: a UUID per request instead of `req-1`, `req-2`, … (which started again at every restart), or a safe incoming `X-Request-Id` from a proxy (at most 64 letters, digits and `. _ : -`); every response returns it in `X-Request-Id`, and error reports carry it as `request_id`. See [Install – Request ids](docs/INSTALL.md#request-ids-and-error-answers).
- **`/api/ready` names no database detail**: the public readiness probe answered with the raw database error. A failing database or schedule check now says `Database not available` / `Schedules could not be read`; the error is logged once (until the check passes again) and shown to administrators on _System status_.
- **Dashboard header**: _data from …_ names the **oldest** source of the dashboard (in its source filter), so one tenant that stopped updating is no longer hidden behind fresh ones.
- **_Test connection_ no longer reports a sign-in page as a working connection**: a base URL that redirects to a login page, or answers with an HTML page where a connector expects its API, used to pass because every status below 400 counted. Now a redirect left over, an HTML page after a redirect, and – for the connectors with a known API (GitHub, GitLab, Forgejo, Prometheus, Kubernetes, Dependency-Track, CheckMk, Gatus, endoflife.date, Insights Hub, Industrial Edge) – any answer that is not JSON fail, with where it redirected to and what to check (_is the base URL the API, and reachable without browser sign-in?_). A Prometheus must answer `{"status":"success"}`. The result shows who answered (`content-type`, `server`, `via`, `www-authenticate`, `location`), and an empty body as _(empty response)_ instead of `{}`.
- **Credentials that are never sent are pointed out**: with authentication _None_, a username/password or token entered for a platform was stored but never sent, and the API answered 401 with nothing saying why (the Prometheus and Gatus presets started that way). New platforms now take the authentication from the credential fields filled in (a token: bearer, username and password: basic; the add dialog shows and lets you change it), the platform settings warn about stored credentials that authentication _None_ does not send and offer to switch, and a 401/403 in the connection test says _Authentication is set to "none" – the username/password you entered are not sent_. Presets and connector types may declare per credential field which authentication it is for (`secretFields[].auth`).
- **GitLab errors say what happened and why**: a redirect (`HTTP 302`) now names where it went and that the base URL must be the API (`https://HOST/api/v4`) reachable without browser sign-in; validation messages no longer read `[object Object]`, and `error`/`error_description` (e.g. `insufficient_scope`) are shown; a non-JSON answer is called an HTML page or quoted (shortened, credentials masked); a 401 lists the likely causes (expired or revoked token, deploy or feed token, a proxy that removes the header) and a 404 of a project mentions reverse proxies that decode `%2F`. GitHub and Forgejo answers get the same treatment.
- **GitLab tokens are sent as `PRIVATE-TOKEN`**, GitLab's own header for personal, group and project access tokens, instead of `Authorization: Bearer`, which reverse proxies and sign-in gateways in front of GitLab often consume (a 401 with an empty answer). For OAuth tokens set the new _Header for the token_ of bearer authentication to `Authorization`; other platforms can use it for APIs that expect the bare token in a header of their own.
- **One unreadable repository file no longer fails the whole repository read**: stack files are requested with `Accept: */*` (GitLab could answer `406` to `application/json`), and a file that still cannot be read is left out and logged.
- **Prometheus checks and objectives**: queries are sent as `POST /api/v1/query` (form-encoded, so long queries no longer hit URL limits; `GET` when the server answers 405). `+Inf` and `-Inf` results are judged against the levels instead of counting as unknown (only `NaN` is). Errors say what answered: on 404 that there is no Prometheus API at `<base>/api/v1/query` and which prefix Mimir, Cortex, VictoriaMetrics or Grafana Cloud need; on a `401 no org id` to add the default header `X-Scope-OrgID`; on other 401/403 the authentication in use; non-JSON answers with their content type and start (credentials masked). New setup guide: [Prometheus](docs/PROMETHEUS.md).
- **The AWS connection test checks what Steward reads**: it only called STS `GetCallerIdentity`, which needs no permission and so always passed. It now also probes tagging `GetResources` (first region), Cost Explorer `GetCostAndUsage` (one day) and IAM `ListUsers` (one item), shows ✓/✗ per part and names the missing IAM action (_the policy of … lacks ce:GetCostAndUsage_); it passes when STS works and lists the rest as warnings. A failed `AssumeRole` says that the caller needs `sts:AssumeRole` and the role's trust policy must trust it. [AWS](docs/AWS.md#policies) now has the caller policy, the role's trust policy with `sts:ExternalId`, the read policy with the access-review actions, and why S3 backups use a platform of their own.
- **Process `http` steps have an answer size limit**: an answer was read into memory whole, however large, and stored in the run in full. Each answer (each page) may now be at most 20 MB – per step `maxResponseMb`, at most 200 – and a larger one fails the step with _The answer of GET /path is larger than 20 MB … read it in pages or raise maxResponseMb_. Outputs above 4 MB are stored in the run as a shortened copy marked `__truncated` (the run itself uses the full value; a resumed run reads such a GET again). Size errors of platform calls no longer show the query of the URL.
- **Browser steps say when the outbound policy blocked a navigation**: a page that navigated or redirected to a host outside the policy (typically a portal sending the browser to a sign-in page elsewhere) failed with a bare `net::ERR_BLOCKED_BY_CLIENT`, or – for an HTTP redirect, which Chromium follows without asking – was not stopped at all. The step now names the origin and the fix (_blocked by the outbound policy: https://sso.example.com – add it to the platform's trusted origins or the allow-list_) in its error, its output (`blockedByPolicy`) and the run log, and leaves a page it was redirected to outside the policy.
- **AWS access reviews go through the outbound policy** like the Git and Snowflake sources (the base URL and the IAM endpoint, which counts as the AWS platform's own).
- **Which outbound rule checks which call is documented**, with a _Do I need an allow-list entry?_ table ([security hardening](docs/SECURITY-HARDENING.md#which-rule-checks-which-call)) and a short help text under _Settings → Security & sign-in → Outbound access_. [Git platforms](docs/GIT-PLATFORMS.md) no longer says the policy must allow the host for reading repositories – built-in connector reads need no entry.
- **Snowflake's `390144 JWT token is invalid` names the likely cause**: the connection test and access reviews add that the variable `account` must be the account locator without region and cloud (`XY12345`) or `ORGNAME-ACCOUNTNAME` with a hyphen – and say so concretely when it contains a dot –, that `user` must be the login name and the user's `RSA_PUBLIC_KEY` the platform's key ([Snowflake](docs/SNOWFLAKE.md#connect-an-account)).
- **Example library**: _Access review 2_ revoked access after a single approval – it now needs a second person (four-eyes); _Add users to a group_ documents the memberships it adds; every example says when to run it ("Recommended schedule"); `staleAfterMinutes` is about twice the schedule everywhere (it was 1× for the weekly end-of-life overview and the monthly privileged review, and too small or too large for 14, 51, 52, 54 and 81) and set for 78, 79, 83 and 86; `# steward-min-version` on the examples that need a newer release (77–79, 82–84, 86 and the new ones); consistent names (_Gatus – Diagnose an endpoint outage_, capitals after the dash) and the shared tag `access-review`; the monitor example 40 no longer names the demo platform. The dataset keys `gatus-uptime` and `eol-overview` keep their names, so existing dashboards keep working.
- **examples/README.md** lists every example, dashboard and monitor, and says which dashboards stay empty in the demo until a review is run.

## [0.3.0] - 2026-11-02

Steward can now be shared by several teams. Processes, platforms, the service catalogue, records, credentials, monitors, dashboards and ticket references can have **owning teams**: members work with them in the roles they have in that team, everyone else does not find them, and _Why do I have access?_ explains every right. Teams get their **own settings** – a Teams or webhook channel for messages about their resources, defaults for new resources, a daily summary for members and their own report branding – and _Settings_ is split into areas with stable addresses. MCP clients can **sign in with OAuth 2.1** instead of a pasted token (optional, off by default), and an AI assistant can **propose a company's own control catalogue and its mappings**, which people review before anything applies. PDFs are drawn in a worker thread with time and memory limits, the web app runs on React 19 and Mantine 9, and a security review before the release closed the remaining gaps of team scopes. Resources without owning teams behave exactly as before. Five database migrations, all additive: `teams-001-scopes`, `teams-002-settings`, `teams-003-defaults`, `mcp-oauth-001` and `proposals-001`. **Check before updating:** audit checkpoint e-mails to addresses outside Steward now need an allowed e-mail domain (_Settings → Notifications → Allowed e-mail domains_), and `/api/settings` is deprecated in favour of `/api/settings/instance`.

### Added

- **AI-assisted import of your own control set** (#74): an AI assistant connected through MCP converts a company's control document (ISMS policy, group requirements, customer lists) into a control catalogue and suggests which Steward items are evidence for which control (`propose_control_catalogue`, `propose_control_mappings`, with `list_control_catalogues`, `get_control_catalogue` and `find_mapping_targets`). Nothing applies directly: proposals wait under **AI requests** for up to 14 days, compared with the catalogue of the same key (new, changed, missing controls). Administrators edit, untick and accept catalogues; everyone who may map controls accepts mappings, which then show who proposed and who accepted them. Large proposals arrive in parts of 200. Off by default: _Settings → MCP server → Let assistants propose control catalogues and mappings_. Migration `proposals-001` (two columns). Guide: [Compliance](docs/COMPLIANCE.md#proposals-from-an-ai-assistant).
- **Team-scoped access, part 1** (#131): processes, platforms and the service catalogue (services, applications, systems) can have **owning teams**. Members of an owning team have their instance roles and the **roles they have in that team** on it (a viewer can be a designer in team Orders); everyone else sees the resource only through an access list entry (an exception for sharing across teams), and administrators have every right, auditors may view. A resource without an owning team behaves exactly as before. In the catalogue, members of an owning team see the item and change it when they are designers there, its named owners as before; an application without teams of its own belongs to the teams of its services; others do not find it in lists, details or link pickers. The web app shows the owning teams on every such page, with _Why do I have access?_, and team roles in the team editor. One policy module (`server/src/auth/policy.ts`) decides and explains: `GET /api/access/explain?kind&id[&user]` names where each right comes from ("designer in team Orders", "access list user:dave", "instance role auditor"). Owning teams are set with `PUT /api/owning-teams/{kind}/{id}` (administrators; adding a team is confirmed with a step-up), roles in a team with `memberRoles` on the team; both are audited and move with migration packages through the identity mapping. Migration `teams-001-scopes` (additive: a column and a table, nothing set on existing data).
- **Team-scoped access, part 2** (#131): records and living documents, credentials, monitors, dashboards and ticket references can have owning teams too. New resources get the creator's teams – only teams that already own something, so nothing changes after an upgrade. _Owning teams for the whole service…_ on a service page (and `POST /api/owning-teams/bulk`) changes the service, its applications, linked records, ticket references and their credentials at once, with a preview of who gains or loses visibility; adding teams needs a step-up. An SSO claim mapping can give **roles in its teams**, kept in step at each sign-in. Team-owned items are left out everywhere for others: scorecard, duties, objectives, document scope, ticket tiles and report sections, the incident review timeline, evidence packs, Git sync, the daily summary and the start page. An application that is also part of a service without owning teams stays visible to everyone. Guide: [Teams](docs/TEAMS.md).
- **Team settings** (#136): _Users → Teams → settings_ of a team, each setting showing _Instance default_ or _Overrides the instance default_:
  - **the team's own channel** – a Teams Workflow or JSON webhook that gets messages about the team's resources (runs, monitors, credentials, documents, duties, objectives, end of life, tickets, access checks, MCP requests), **also** or **instead of** the instance channel; the URL is encrypted with the vault and never returned;
  - **new resources of members** – get the team _once it owns something_ (default), _always_ or _never_; members choose one of their teams in their profile;
  - **daily summary for members** who have not saved their own;
  - **report branding** – company name, accent colour, classification, footer note and logo, for the team's report schedules, run reports of its processes and PDFs of its records.

  Administrators change them, members read them, changes are audited (`scope: team:<name>`, never with the URL), and migration packages carry them. API: `GET/PUT /api/teams/{id}/settings`, `…/settings/test`, `…/settings/logo`, `GET/PUT /api/me/new-resource-teams`, `DELETE /api/me/daily-summary`. Migrations `teams-002-settings` and `teams-003-defaults` (additive). Guide: [Settings](docs/SETTINGS.md).

- **Optional OAuth 2.1 sign-in for MCP clients** (#12): clients that support the MCP authorization specification sign in with the person's Steward account (password or SSO) instead of a pasted token – discovery (RFC 9728, RFC 8414), self-registration (RFC 7591, can be turned off in favour of clients administrators add), a consent page with a step-up, PKCE `S256`, access tokens bound to the MCP endpoint only (RFC 8707), rotating refresh tokens with reuse detection and revocation (RFC 7009). People see and revoke their connected clients in their profile, administrators every connection under _Settings → Security & sign-in → MCP server_. **Off by default**; MCP tokens keep working either way. Migration `mcp-oauth-001` (three new tables). Guide: [MCP](docs/MCP.md#sign-in-with-oauth-optional).
- **KPI widgets can add up their sources**: with several sources (for example three Insights Hub tenants) a KPI shows one value per source as before, or – _With several sources → Sum of all sources_ in the widget editor, `combine: sum` in YAML – their sum as one number, with its change over the period, a sparkline and the values per source below it. With one source selected in the dashboard filter it shows that source's value.

### Changed

- **Settings are split into areas with their own addresses** (#136): _General_, _Security & sign-in_, _Integrations_, _Notifications_, _Appearance & branding_, _Catalogue & compliance_ and _Maintenance_ (`/settings/<area>`); each says whose settings they are, the command palette finds a setting by name, and _Save_ is enabled only after a change. Old links (`/settings`, `/settings#notifications`) still work. No setting changes value. A table of every setting with its scope: [docs/SETTINGS.md](docs/SETTINGS.md).
- **Web app on React 19 and Mantine 9** (#49): the look stays as it was (the light colour variant and the corner radius are kept; a hovered notification pauses only its own timer). Select fields are announced as combo boxes, as the ARIA pattern describes, and switches now show a mark in their thumb. The first page load is about 40 kB larger (gzip), from the newer React and Mantine.
- **Route access is declared on the route and checked before the body is read** (#16): whether a route is public, a webhook, for signed-in sessions only (no API tokens), for certain roles, or open to status viewers is now part of the route's configuration instead of lists in `app.ts`. One check reads it for every request, before the request body is parsed, so a caller who may not use a route gets 401/403 without their upload or JSON being read. 65 administrator-only routes (settings, users, teams, single sign-on, backups, migration, theme, report branding, notification settings) now refuse other roles this way. The sign-in check (`me()`) and `forbid()` exist once, in `server/src/auth/access.ts`, instead of in 17 modules. The route permission test compares the declarations with its table. Status viewers may log out, change their password and confirm their sign-in, as they could before.
- **Development: vitest 5 and a coverage report** (#24, #23, #22): the server tests run on vitest 5. `npm run test:coverage` measures coverage; CI does so on Node 24, lists the totals and the least covered files in the job summary and keeps the HTML report for 14 days (a report, no minimum yet). Browser-test servers start on a free port of their own choosing and report it in their start line.
- **Coverage says "not visible" instead of "gap" for evidence you may not see** (#21): the Controls page and the read API now use the rule of evidence packs. A control with evidence in the period that the reader may not see shows _Not visible (n hidden)_; a covered control says how many more items are hidden. **API:** `GET /api/v1/control-coverage` returns the new `status` value `notVisible` and a `hidden` count; a client that accepts only `covered`, `gap` and `unmapped` has to accept it.
- **PDFs are drawn in a worker thread** (#13): run reports, summary and scheduled reports, PDFs of records and the summary of evidence packs are drawn apart from the thread that answers requests, one at a time, stopped after 60 s (`PDF_TIMEOUT`) or 512 MB (`PDF_TOO_LARGE`); with 20 waiting, the next one is refused (`PDF_BUSY`). The data is collected first; drawing reads no database. `SM_PDF_WORKER=off` draws in the main thread as before; with `auto` (default) Steward falls back to that when the worker cannot start. _System status_ shows which applies. See [Reports](docs/REPORTS.md#how-pdfs-are-created).
- **Audit checkpoint e-mails follow the allowed e-mail domains** (#18): auditors' addresses outside Steward need an allowed domain (_Settings → Notifications → Allowed e-mail domains_), as report recipients and credential owners do; saving a recipient with another domain is refused (`EMAIL_DOMAIN_NOT_ALLOWED`). **Check before updating:** add the domain of an external auditor or archive mailbox to the allowed domains, or its checkpoint e-mails stop.
- **Development: shared helpers and smaller route files** (#18): one CSV writer (`server/src/csv.ts`; the audit, credential and evidence-pack CSV files are unchanged), one e-mail address rule (`isEmailAddress` in `shared`) for profiles, credential owners, service contacts, report recipients, record signers and auditors, and one way out for e-mail (`notifications/email.ts`). The route registration (`routes/index.ts`) and the read API (`api/v1/routes.ts`) are split by area, with the same routes, access rules and OpenAPI document.

### Deprecated

- `GET/PUT /api/settings` (#136): use `GET/PUT /api/settings/instance`. The old path answers with `Deprecation: true` and a `Link` to the new one, and is removed in 0.4.0.

### Fixed

- **Owning teams also apply to maintenance windows, status pages and linking applications** (security review before the release, #131):
  - A maintenance window for items of a team is planned, changed and cancelled only by those who may change them. Other people neither see it in the list and calendar nor find the names of its items.
  - A status page shows a team's service only once someone who may change the service adds it.
  - Adding an application of a team to a service of the whole instance (which makes it everyone's) is for administrators.
  - Items without owning teams behave as before.
  - Owning teams of deleted credentials, dashboards, platforms, systems, monitors and tickets are removed with them, so a new dashboard of the same name does not inherit them.
- **A team's own notification channel** asks the administrator to confirm with a step-up when its URL is set or replaced, as the instance's channels do.
- **MCP sign-in and AI proposals are harder to misuse:**
  - Self-registered OAuth clients are counted apart from those administrators add, and unused registrations are removed after an hour.
  - Angle brackets are dropped from client names (the consent page shows the name inside a sentence).
  - Proposals are limited to 2 MB each and 8 MB per person.
  - The _AI requests_ list shows what a proposal is about without its whole comparison.
  - A proposed mapping to an item the reviewer may not see shows neither the item nor the AI's reason for it.
- **OAuth access of MCP clients ends for good** (#12): deactivating a person or resetting their password (by an administrator, or with `steward admin reset-password`) revokes their connected clients, and turning OAuth off revokes everyone's. Before, the access came back when the person was reactivated or OAuth turned on again. MCP tokens are unchanged: they are refused while their owner is deactivated, and revoked by their owner or an administrator.
- **PDFs:** when the worker thread cannot start, Steward tries again after 1 minute, then less often up to every hour, instead of drawing in the main thread until the next restart. Evidence images in a run report get thumbnails up to 10 MB each and 64 MB together (images are not counted in the 512 MB memory limit of the worker); larger ones stay in the evidence list without a thumbnail.
- **Steward's own PDFs are not refused when many PDFs are waiting:** the run report and the access-change record after a run, and scheduled reports, wait for their turn. Before, a burst of requests could leave a run without its access-change entries.
- **CSV downloads of dashboard tables** defuse text that a spreadsheet would run as a formula, as the other CSV files do. E-mail addresses with control characters are refused.
- **Accessible names contain the visible text** (WCAG 2.5.3 _Label in Name_, checked by axe 4.14): the search button in the header, the user menu, the start-page tiles, the language switch on the sign-in page, the _Document this change_ and _Dismiss_ buttons of access reconciliation and the _Diff_ button of document editions are announced with the words they show (for example _Dismiss: alice_), so voice control and screen readers use the same name as the screen.
- **The link to a status page without sign-in works** (#26): the link the web app showed after _Link without sign-in_ pointed into the API and carried part of the creator's browser session (`…/api/?_sk=…/status#t=…`), so opening it elsewhere answered _Please sign in_. It is now `https://<host>[/<path prefix>]/status#t=…`. The part of the session in old links is useless without the creator's browser cookie; to be safe, create a new link (this replaces the old one) and sign out once in the browser that created it. The browser test now opens the link exactly as the page shows it.

## [0.2.10] - 2026-10-31

AI assistants can now ask for changes instead of only reading: a changing platform call or a run requested through MCP becomes a request that a person confirms – per platform by the token owner or, four-eyes, by an approver – and runs once, as its requester. **Service status pages** show colleagues outside the service team how their services are doing – from the monitors, maintenance windows, objectives and known vendor issues, without names or tickets – to a new status viewer role or through a secret link without sign-in. Dashboards get a **Vega-Lite chart widget** for the few views the built-in widgets do not cover, drawn from the widget's dataset only and without `unsafe-eval` in the Content Security Policy. Two database migrations, both additive: `ops-012-mcp-requests` and `catalogue-010-status-pages`.

### Added

- **Vega-Lite chart widget** (#120): a dashboard widget `vega` draws a [Vega-Lite](https://vega.github.io/vega-lite/) spec from its dataset, for the few views the built-in widgets do not cover – with a table view, the theme's colours (schemes `steward` and `steward-status`) and a spec editor that checks as you type. Only the dataset is shown: specs that load data or images (`url`), link (`href`) or carry their own data are refused when saving. Expressions run in vega-interpreter, so the Content Security Policy stays without `unsafe-eval`, and the chart chunk is loaded only on dashboards that use it. Examples on _Edge fleet_ (devices per firmware version) and _Tenant usage_ (used share of each limit) ([docs/DASHBOARDS.md](docs/DASHBOARDS.md#vega-lite-charts)). New dependencies: `vega`, `vega-lite`, `vega-interpreter` (web).
- **Service status pages** (#26): read-only pages for people outside the service team – per service the state from its linked monitors (counts only, no names), planned and running maintenance, known issues with vendors (the vendor and since when, never the ticket) and optionally the availability objectives, plus a notice. A new role _status_ (status viewer) sees nothing but the status pages, enforced once on the server for every route. A page can get a secret link without sign-in, stored as a hash, kept in the URL fragment so no log sees it, revocable and audited ([docs/STATUS-PAGES.md](docs/STATUS-PAGES.md)). Migration `catalogue-010-status-pages` (additive).
- **Approved writes through MCP** (#11): an AI assistant can ask for a changing platform call (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE) through the MCP gateway, or to start a run – it never happens when it asks. Each becomes a request under _AI requests_ that the token owner confirms or, per platform, a second person (an approver or administrator who may use the platform) approves; it then runs once, as the token owner, with the rights and settings of that moment, and expires after 24 hours otherwise. Changing calls are off per platform (turning them on needs a fresh confirmation of the sign-in), run requests are off for the instance. Decisions only with a signed-in session, new notification event _Request from an AI assistant_, every step audited, `get_operation_request` and `request_run` tools ([docs/MCP.md](docs/MCP.md#approved-writes)). Migration `ops-012-mcp-requests` (additive).

### Fixed

- Two example dashboard titles lost the text after their comma (_Users, assets and agents over time (%)_ on _Tenant usage_, _Critical, high and failing policies_ on _Vulnerabilities_); a test now catches unknown widget keys in the examples.

## [0.2.9] - 2026-10-31

Steward reads the vulnerabilities of the software it runs and reviews one person's access across platforms at a time. **Dependency-Track** projects can be named on any application component: Steward reads their open findings and policy violations, records new critical findings in the application's history, adds the part _Vulnerabilities_ to the service scorecard and a monitor check type. **Person access reviews** collect everything one person may do across several access checks – read live, frozen, decided right by right, approved by a second person and sealed – and check later that the rights to remove are gone; approved reviews count as evidence of their access checks. AWS IAM becomes an access check source. Three database migrations, all additive: `catalogue-009-dependency-track`, `scorecard-004-vulnerabilities` and `compliance-003-person-reviews`.

### Added

- **Dependency-Track** (#119): a platform type _Dependency-Track_ (API key). Any application component names its project – `name@version`, `name@latest` or the UUID; every 6 hours or on _Read now_ Steward reads the open findings by severity (suppressed ones and those analysed as not affected or false positive do not count), the policy violations, the risk score and the most important findings with EPSS and the fixed version. A new critical finding or failing policy goes into the application's history. The service scorecard gets the part _Vulnerabilities_, and monitors the check type `dependency-track` (critical and high findings, failing policies). A demo instance ([docs/DEPENDENCY-TRACK.md](docs/DEPENDENCY-TRACK.md)). Migrations `catalogue-009-dependency-track` and `scorecard-004-vulnerabilities` (additive).
- **Person access reviews** (#118): _Compliance → Person reviews_ collects everything one person may do across several access checks – read live (or from the latest snapshot), matched by their user names and e-mail addresses, and frozen. The reviewer decides keep or remove per right (a removal needs a reason, nobody reviews their own access), a second person approves or sends it back, and the review is sealed with a SHA-256 over all decisions that can be verified. _Check removals_ reads the platforms again and closes the review when every right to remove is gone. Approved reviews are evidence of their access checks on controls and in evidence packs. New access check source _AWS IAM_ (users with groups, managed and inline policies, technical users marked) ([docs/COMPLIANCE.md](docs/COMPLIANCE.md#person-access-reviews)). Migration `compliance-003-person-reviews` (additive).

## [0.2.8] - 2026-10-30

Steward signs in to more platforms and looks after itself. Platforms can sign in with a **key-pair JWT** (Snowflake, Google service accounts) or **AWS request signing** (SigV4, written out and checked against AWS's own examples – no AWS SDK), also as an assumed role. Steward makes **encrypted backups to S3** on a schedule: a consistent copy of the database and the data folder, encrypted with a backup passphrase that is never stored, kept by a grandfather–father–son rule and tested by a weekly restore test that decrypts and checks every file and the audit chain. **AWS accounts** show the resources, costs and IAM keys of each application by its tag; **Snowflake** accounts show databases, failing tasks and warehouse credits, feed access reviews through the role hierarchy and a monitor check. Two database migrations, both additive: `ops-011-backup-runs` and `catalogue-008-cloud-readouts`.

### Added

- **Platform sign-in: key-pair JWT and AWS request signing** (#114). _Key-pair JWT_ signs a short-lived JWT (RS/PS/ES algorithms, at most an hour) with the platform's private key, with templated claims and the public key fingerprint – sent as the token (Snowflake) or exchanged for an access token (RFC 7523). _AWS request signing_ signs every request with Signature Version 4 (checked against AWS's documented examples, no AWS SDK), with an access key or an assumed role (STS AssumeRole, external ID, renewed before it ends), for AWS APIs, S3 and S3-compatible storage. Both work with the connection test, _Get a new token_ and the MCP gateway ([docs/PLATFORM-SIGN-IN.md](docs/PLATFORM-SIGN-IN.md)).
- **Backups to S3** (#117): on a schedule (default 02:15 daily) Steward copies its database – a SQLite snapshot while it runs, or `pg_dump` of the PostgreSQL schema – and its data folder into a ZIP with a SHA-256 manifest, encrypts it with AES-256-GCM in authenticated chunks under a key from a backup passphrase (scrypt; the passphrase is never stored) and uploads it to an S3 bucket or S3-compatible storage through a platform with AWS request signing (multipart for large files). A grandfather–father–son rule keeps 7 daily, 4 weekly and 12 monthly backups; S3 Object Lock is respected. A weekly restore test downloads the newest backup, compares its checksum, decrypts it and checks every file, the SQLite integrity and the audit chain of the snapshot (PostgreSQL: `pg_restore --list`). Failures notify the administrators (new event _Backup failed_); runs, settings and passphrase changes are audited. _Settings → Backups_, the offline tool `npm run backup -- decrypt|verify|info` (desktop: `steward backup …`), `postgresql-client` in the container image and a mock S3 in the demo ([docs/BACKUPS.md](docs/BACKUPS.md)). Migration `ops-011-backup-runs` (additive).
- **AWS accounts** (#116): a platform type _Amazon Web Services (AWS account)_ – one account, signed with SigV4 per service, an access key or an assumed role, the connection test asks STS who it is. Application components of type _Cloud account_ name it with the value of the application tag (`application=shop`): Steward lists the resources with the tag in the configured regions (Resource Groups Tagging API), the costs per month for the tag (Cost Explorer, read at most once a day) and the IAM users with the tag and the age and last use of their access keys, which can be added to the credentials register; every 6 hours or on _Read now_, one shared read per account, resources that came or went in the application's history. A demo account with a mock that checks every signature ([docs/AWS.md](docs/AWS.md)). Migration `catalogue-008-cloud-readouts` (additive).
- **Snowflake** (#115): a platform type _Snowflake (SQL API)_ with key-pair JWT sign-in, statements with bind parameters only, read from the `ACCOUNT_USAGE` views. Application components of type _Snowflake database_ show the tables with size and last change, the tasks that failed in the last day and the credits of the warehouses used (every 6 hours or on _Read now_; a newly failing task in the application's history). Access reviews get the source _Snowflake_: the roles of every user, or who reaches a database, schema or table through the role hierarchy, technical users marked. Monitor check type `snowflake`: freshness of a table (current, from `INFORMATION_SCHEMA`), failing tasks, warehouse credits. A demo account whose mock verifies every JWT ([docs/SNOWFLAKE.md](docs/SNOWFLAKE.md)).

## [0.2.7] - 2026-10-30

Steward reads the platforms applications are built and run on. **GitHub, GitLab and Forgejo/Gitea** become connectors: repository components show the pipeline of the default branch, the latest release, open security alerts, packages, deploy keys and tokens (offered for the credentials register) and the tech stack read from Dockerfile, package.json, go.mod and similar files; a monitor check follows the pipeline, and access reviews read organisation and repository members. **Kafka** topics of applications – Confluent Cloud or any Kafka cluster, read with a read-only service account – show their settings, consumer lag and schema version; monitors check new messages, lag, stuck consumer groups and the schema; a guarded peek reads a few messages for troubleshooting. **MQTT** brokers get a monitor check for messages within a window, silent devices and connected clients, keeping only times and counts per topic. Two database migrations, both additive: `catalogue-007-component-readouts` and `ops-010-mqtt-topics`.

### Added

- **Git platforms** (#107): platform types _GitHub_ (also Enterprise Server), _GitLab_ and _Forgejo / Gitea_ with a read-only token, a connection test and a small API description each. Application components of type _Repository_ name their Git platform and repository path; Steward reads the default branch and its pipeline (workflow runs, GitLab pipelines, commit status), the latest release, open Dependabot alerts, packages and container images, deploy keys and GitLab deploy and access tokens – each can be added to the credentials register after review – and the tech stack from Dockerfile, package.json, go.mod, .nvmrc, .tool-versions, global.json, composer.json and pom.xml (source _read from the repository files_), every 6 hours or on _Read now_. Monitor check type `git` (pipeline of a branch, optional alert limits), access reviews with the source _Git_ (organisation, group or repository members with their role, outside collaborators marked), the example _GitHub – repository health_ with the dashboard _Repositories_, and demo hosts ([docs/GIT-PLATFORMS.md](docs/GIT-PLATFORMS.md)). Migration `catalogue-007-component-readouts` (additive).

- **Kafka** (#112): a platform type _Kafka cluster_ for Confluent Cloud and Platform, Apache Kafka, Redpanda, MSK and Event Hubs – bootstrap servers with SASL (PLAIN, SCRAM, OAUTHBEARER) over TLS through a pure-JavaScript client, the Schema Registry as its base URL. Application components of type _Kafka topic_ name their cluster; Steward reads settings, partitions, the newest message's time, consumer groups with their lag and the schema version every 6 hours or on _Read now_ (a new schema version is recorded in the application's history). Monitor check type `kafka`: new messages within a window, consumer lag, stuck groups, the expected schema version. _Peek_ reads a few recent messages for troubleshooting – off unless the platform allows it, platform managers only, at most 5 messages of 4 KB, sensitive fields hidden, every read audited. Examples _Kafka – topic standards_ (with the dashboard _Kafka topics_) and _Kafka – schema change review_, and a demo cluster ([docs/KAFKA.md](docs/KAFKA.md)).
- **MQTT** (#113): a platform type _MQTT broker_ (mqtt, mqtts, ws, wss; password or client certificate) and the monitor check type `mqtt`: messages on a topic or pattern within a window, devices that went silent (every topic of a pattern), connected clients. One listening connection per broker in use keeps per topic only when the last message came and how many – never the content (table `mqtt_topics`, migration `ops-010-mqtt-topics`, additive). A demo broker over WebSocket ([docs/MQTT.md](docs/MQTT.md)).
- Processes see what Steward read from a component's platform (Kubernetes namespace, repository, Kafka topic) as `readout` of the components of an application input.

### Fixed

- The CheckMk trend example's test depended on the time of day: the demo `/var` series now grows 0.8 % a day, far enough from the 14-day warning.

## [0.2.6] - 2026-10-30

Steward follows slow signals and reads more of the monitoring around it.
